Inthe'light of the above, the following are planned for the coming year:


1.   Sc1·eening of tryptami~e compounds in animals and man for hallucino-

     genic action.
2.   Completion of the study of the use of riboflavin as a trace chemical
     to prove the ingestion of medication in a clinical setting.
3.   Further studies on the use of phenoxybenz.amine and other adrenergic

     blocking compounds as antidotes to-LSD-"25•
4.   Further refinement of the use of quantitative brain wave studies to
     diagnose schiz.ophrenia and to determine the action of hallucinogens.
s.   Selection of one or more of a series of stimulant diamines for chronic
     toxicity studies )n animals so that they can be tested in normal human

     volunteers for possible stimulant action.
6.   A search in brain extracts of rabbits treated with Tofranil (imipra-
                     '
     mine) to determine if any stimulant amine accwnulates as a result of

     degradation of the drug molecule in tl1e body.
7.   Determination of the anti-cholinesterase and aminoxidase inhibitory

     effect of the st~hulant diamines.
8.
            -
     Assay of' acet~l:.!holine
                 .
                               content of various portions of the brain after

     prolonged administration of deanol.
9.   The assay in man of an intravenous analgesic solution composed of
     morphine and choline or morphine and carnit:ine wherein the-.pharma-
     cologically neutral quaternary cornpound prevents the engagement of

     peripheralreceptors by the morphine.

    It is proposed to conduct screening and range finding tests on volunteers··
with chernical compounds of ki1ownbehavloJ·al activity. Studies will be
conducted on derivatives of selected biologically active co~1pounds to deter•
mine their action on the central nervous system of man.

      In addition to these Investigations. the following work will be undertaken:

                 1, Pharmacological testing will be conducted on animals
            utilizing candidate hallucinogenic chcmic~ts.

               2. Studies will be conducted on compounds of reJativly
            simple configuration to assess their ability to inhibit trained
            behavior in animals utilizing the condition avoidance responses.

               3. Studies will be conducted of compounds ln normal ht1man
            vo~unteers for possible behavioral changes utilizing compounds.
            provided by the sponsor.                                 .

                 4. Quantitative studies· or the I-;EG in animals and man to
            determine stimulant or depressant effects or candidate mater•
            ia ls.

              Under .Q. we no.o~ have perfected and plan to use at the New Jersey laboratory a
        quantitative integrator for drug studies_ in both man and aniraals.
              Adult unanesthetized 1 trained animals are used. Under anesthesia small gold·
        plated \"'OOd scre\·lS are used as dural electrodes. These-are lightly engaged in the               •
        calvarium and held firmly in place with methacrylate \-lith the permanent lead
        attached. The recording leads are bi~p-olarcfrom the left and right frontoparietal
        lobes of the brain. The animals are in a sound attenuating box and sit quietly for
        the entire I to 2 hour session after a-few training periods.·
              A Crohoc:ki integra1:or is <Htached to one lead. This device rectifies the brain
        waves and by a chopping process, counts the area under the rectified curve. A
        signal is made at a pre·set degr:ce_of_sgn~JtJyj_ty. The number of signals per unit
        time is a measure of the degree of-ele-c-trical activity or "electrogenesis." The
       ·number of pulses or signals is directly correlatable~tlilh-the area under the curve.
        The constancy of the electrogenesis Hith elapsed lime can be calculated and treated
        by the usual statistical methods. In this type ofanalysis 1 periods as long as
        five minutes can be used as a baseline or control a~d thus transient artefacts are
        elimiMtcd. The action of drugs is usually ll1C9S!Jre..c:J. at J-5 minute intervals after
        intravenous injection and the drug action is expressed as change in percentage
        electrogenesis fran the control preinjection period. AroL:sal or stLmulant drug
        effects are recorded as a decrciase while sleep (sine waves) produce an Increase in
        electrogenesis.

         inhibits the participants.
              The participants Hill be limited to 12 or 15. The record will be in the form
         of the individual's er.m notes and the notes of a scientifically trained secretary.
         The Suio.iarizing secretary uill chunge Hith each three-hour session. In a two-day
         conference we \·:ould have five sessions of three hours each.                        ·        1J

          The experimental subjects are a group of sixteen volunteers 1 inmates of the
     U. s. federal Penitentiary at Atlanta, Georgia. Healthy and emotionally stable (as
     determined by psychometric tests) individual$ are chosen. Their mean age at present
    _is 34 years., their mean weight is 160 pounds. Their J.Q.s. range frc;m 90 to 123.
     Thedoses of drugs are administered at-randcm at weekly intervals. All subjec.ts
     have experienced the effects of central nervous system stimulants and dep.ressants.
--- -they are able to identify and to cc.tmJuni c.a te the subject i "e response.s to these drug~
     and have proven that their responses are sonewhat reproducible.

        Tests are carried out iq" a hospital r0011. The subjects are enc.ouraged to stay
  in bed during the trials. Dl:iigs are administered orally, two hours after a light-
  morning breakfast. Testins      is
                                   usually done single or double bli,,d. The-drugs~are-:c-~
  handed to each subject in a small labeled bottle. The label bears only the name of
  the subject. The subjects place the tablets-or capsules-in their mouth under
  supe.rvision of the physician and wash them down with a cup of quinine flavored water.
  The evaluation of ·the drug effects~is=-b~~ed on tl'\r~~ t)'pe! of responses.

  A.   Observations are made by the attending doctor during a three-hour test period
       following administration of~the drug. TheSe inc1udemeasurements of blood pres-
       sure, pulse rate 1 body t~~perature and hand steadiness at 60 or 90 minutes
       intervals. It also includes observations on the neurological and mental status_
       of the subjects, such as alertness, sle£'p and arousal fran sleep.

  B.   A personal interview is made at the end of the three-hour period. In this inter-
       view the subject is encouraged to_te1Lbis_ {~!'!_lings under the influence of the
       drug and conpares_them_witb similar or dissimilar effe~ts which he may have ex-
       perienced with the other drugs on previous ~rlals.

  c.   Twenty-four hour state~ent. The subjects prepore a written statement of the ef-
       fects experienced in the twenty-four hour period after the administration of the
       drug. The criteria for the _eff~c_t_s w~r-~ o;stal:>l ished by the subjects themselves.
       Subjects were told either after the interview or after they had prepared their
       written statci.'lent which drugal)d dose had been a.dministered •. This increases
       their degree Qf drug ~IJP!li.~ticatron an•rallO..:·i thelllTo evaluate new drug effects.

tenuous since identification has been made only by Reineche precipitation which
method only tests for quaternary nitrogen containing ccmpo~nds. A few of the optically
active acids which might. be esterified with choline are lactic 1 pantotheni' and as-
